## 97. Dimension 17 closed: K' is a group, so Delsarte's LP applies (2026-08-25)

`tau(17) = 5346 + 2*alpha` with `alpha = alpha(Cay(K', W_4))` (sections 74-74i).  Section 75
left `192 <= alpha <= 193` and called it the one live lead in the low range.  **`alpha = 192`
exactly, so `tau(17) = 5730` and there is no dimension-17 record in the layered family.**

**The mistake was in how the object was being read, not in the search.**  For four sections
`K'` was handled as a 1024-vertex vertex-transitive graph and fed to CP-SAT, which never
separated 192 from 193 -- three runs of the same capped model returned dual bounds 192, 193 and
195, and the deterministic single-worker variant returned 238 (section 75's caveat).  But `K'`
is a **linear code**: it is `{b in RM(2,4) : |b n S_0| even}`, closed under XOR, hence an
elementary abelian group `F_2^10`, and `W_4 = -W_4` is a symmetric subset of it.  So
`G = Cay(K', W_4)` is a translation association scheme and **Delsarte's LP bound applies
verbatim** -- for a Cayley graph on an abelian group it is exactly Lovasz's `theta'`.  It
returns **192 on the nose**.

**The certificate, in six numbers.**  For `g : K' -> Q` with non-negative Fourier transform,

    sum_{c,c' in C} g(c-c') = (1/1024) sum_y ghat(y) |sum_{c in C} chi_y(c)|^2 >= 0 ,

so if also `g(x) <= -1` off `W_4 u {0}` then `|C| g(0) - |C|(|C|-1) >= 0`, i.e.
`|C| <= 1 + g(0)`.  Index the characters by the eigenvalue `lambda(y) = sum_{w in W_4} chi_y(w)`
and by whether `y` is trivial on the fibre subgroup `H`:

| (lambda, trivial on H) | (30,no) | (12,yes) | (12,no) | (6,no) | (-4,no) | (-2,no) | rest |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| characters | 16 | 10 | 120 | 160 | 360 | 240 | 118 |
| `b_y` | 65/80 | 40/80 | 30/80 | 28/80 | 10/80 | 9/80 | 0 |
| mass | 13 | 5 | 45 | 56 | 45 | 27 | 0 |

`g(0) = sum_y b_y = 191`, and `g <= -1` at all 963 points off `W_4 u {0}` -- 887 at exactly
`-1`, 60 at `-21/5`, 16 at `-9`.  Scaled by 80 every quantity is an integer, so the check is one
Walsh-Hadamard transform with no floating point: `verifications/closed/dim17-layered-family/
scripts/theta17.py`, about 15 s, numpy only, no solver.  The lower bound is Cohn-Li's own six
pairwise non-adjacent fibres, all 18336 pairs checked.

**Why every earlier relaxation stopped at 256, and why they were the same 256.**  The spectrum
of `G` is `60^1 30^16 12^130 6^160 (-2)^240 (-4)^375 (-10)^96 (-20)^6`, so the Hoffman ratio
bound is `1024*20/80 = 256` -- numerically identical to the clique-cover bound `8 * 32` of
section 74c, and for the same reason: both are satisfied by the uniform fractional point
`x = 1/4` on the 32 fibres.  Clique cuts, block-pair cuts and odd-hole cuts all admit that
point, so **no cut family expressed on the quotient `Q` can ever beat 256**; section 74c
observed this empirically and blamed `omega(Q) = 4`.  The LP over the group does not factor
through `Q` at all, which is exactly why it sees more.  (A dual restricted to `g = -1` off
`W_4` -- 60 unknowns -- also returns 256; the strength comes from letting `g` dip below `-1`,
which it does at 76 of the 963 points.)

**What this retires.**  `cap22.py`, `cap22_det.py`, `cap28.py`, `decide193.py`, `finish17.py`
and the `P(t)` cap of section 74g are no longer part of any argument.  Sections 74e-74h are
still correct and still interesting -- the full-fibre split, the `a_c <= 22` cap, the exact
`alpha(G[R(c)]) = 160` -- but they are now a longer road to a settled number.

**Scope is unchanged from section 75.**  This closes the layered family over a `Lambda_16`
equator with an antipodal pole pair and integral tiers, which by section 74i is every usable
variant of Cohn-Li's construction.  It is not an unconditional `tau(17) <= 5730`.

**How to apply.**  Before treating a combinatorial optimum as a search problem, ask whether the
ground set is a group and the forbidden set is symmetric.  If it is, the answer is an LP with an
exactly checkable dual, and the search was never necessary.  The tell here was in plain sight
for four sections: `K'` was *defined* by two linear conditions.  The same question is worth
asking of every remaining `alpha` in this repository -- section 97a does exactly that.

### 97a. The same question asked everywhere else (2026-08-25)

The uncomfortable part of section 97 is that **the technique was already in this repository**.
`verifications/closed/dim17-23-cohn-li-mechanism/scripts/lpbound.py` has run the Delsarte LP on
a Cayley graph on `F_2^10` since the dimension-19 work, with the abelian/theta' equivalence
written in its own docstring.  It was never pointed at dimension 17.  So the remaining `alpha`s
were all re-examined:

| bottleneck | ground set a group? | ratio bound | LP bound | truth | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| dim 17 `alpha(Cay(K', W_4))` | **yes**, `F_2^10` | 256 | **192** | 192 | **tight -- closed** |
| dim 19 `alpha(Cay(K,S))` | yes, `F_2^10` | 391 | 384 | 320 (section 72) | loose by 20% |
| dim 19 `alpha(G_0)` | yes, `F_2^9` | 192 | 192 | 160 (section 72) | loose by 20%; LP = ratio bound |
| LEECH class, 248 lines | no | -- | 425.45 | 248 | closed; the Gamma_72 analogue is below |
| dims 9-19 maximality | not an `alpha` | -- | -- | -- | settled by sweep |

The pattern in that table is the useful part: **the LP is worth running exactly when it beats
the ratio bound.** At dimension 17 it does, by 25%, and it is then exact. At both dimension-19
graphs it returns the ratio bound to the decimal and is 20% loose. So the cheap pre-check is to
compute `lambda_min` first: if `n(-lambda)/(d-lambda)` is already the answer you would have
guessed, the LP will not rescue you.

**Dimension 19 keeps its solver, and that is fine.** Section 72's CP-SAT run returned
**OPTIMAL** -- it exhausted the search -- which is a different object from section 75's
time-limited dual bound on a capped model. An exhausted search gives the same answer on every
run; a dual bound at a time limit is whatever the clock allowed, which is how dimension 17
produced 192, 193, 195 and 238 from one model. **Distinguish the two before trusting either:
"OPTIMAL" is a proof, "best bound so far" is a stopwatch reading.**

**Dimension 19 is why nobody tried this at 17.**  There the LP gives 384 against the true 320 --
loose enough to look like a dead end, and the section-72 proof went the combinatorial route
instead.  The two problems look alike and behave completely differently: at 17 the LP is exact,
at 19 it is 20% off.  There is no way to tell in advance which you have; the LP costs fifteen
seconds, so run it.

**The class problem is worth one more look but is not obviously available.**  The row above
is the LEECH's class problem -- 425.45 bounds any line system in R^24 with |cos| <= 1/4 and
owes nothing to the Leech, and 248 is Ma et al.'s record in it
(`closed/class-problem-upper-bound/`).  Gamma_72's class problem is a different one, with no
bound of any kind, and it is the one that moves 23 dimensions.  The lines of
Gamma_72 *are* in bijection with a subset of an abelian group: two minimal vectors are congruent
mod `2*Gamma_72` exactly when they are equal or antipodal (`|u-v|^2 in {0,32}` forces
`<u,v> = +-8`), so the 3 109 087 800 lines inject into `Gamma_72/2*Gamma_72 = F_2^72`.  And the
class condition is nearly a difference condition: `|<u,v>| <= 2` iff
`min(|u-v|^2, |u+v|^2) >= 12`, with `u-v` and `u+v` in the same coset.  What breaks the analogy
is that the coset does not determine which pair of its elements is `(u-v, u+v)`, so
`|<u,v>|` is **not** a function of the group difference and there is no forbidden set to feed
Delsarte.  The sufficient direction does hold -- cosets of minimum norm `>= 12` give valid pairs
-- but that constructs classes rather than bounding them.  Recorded here so the next person does
not have to rediscover why it fails.

**The habit to keep.**  Every time an `alpha` or `omega` in this project stops responding to a
solver, the first question is now: is the ground set closed under a group operation, and is the
forbidden set symmetric?  Twice out of five it was, and once that was the whole answer.

## 98. The dual VERTEX, not a rounding of it: dimension 68 raised by 97 (2026-08-26)

`run3` proposes a dual with a floating-point solve, rounds it to rationals and repairs it into
exact feasibility by lowering one coordinate.  Sound -- an imprecise dual weakens the bound and
cannot inflate it -- but LOSSY, and the loss depends on the conditioning, which depends on how
the Gram happens to be presented.  That is the whole of the "basis-dependence" recorded above.

Two steps remove it, both in `common/exact_vertex.py`:

* **Reduce.**  Most rows are identifications, not equations: `n[c] = n[-c]`, and translations
  that read `n[c] = n[c']`, `n[c] = 0` or `n[c] = 1`.  Substituting them out is exact, and takes
  k = 4 from 3473 rows x 1681 columns to 617 x 520 -- dense, well scaled, and then every HiGHS
  method and presolve setting returns the SAME optimum, where before they disagreed by 0.16%.
* **Take the vertex.**  Complementary slackness makes the optimal dual tight exactly on the
  active columns, so it solves a rational linear system.  Read the active set off the numeric
  dual, solve in Fractions, then VERIFY `A^T y <= c` over every column exactly.  A wrong active
  set fails the check and nothing is returned.

Result: **tau(68) >= 361 275 480**, the exact optimum of the programme -- the numeric primal
attains it, so no dual can do better and the claim cannot be improved without a different
programme.  Validated by reproducing tau(70) = 1 249 778 250 and tau(69) = 627 822 180 to the
unit, and the Leech's 60-degree two-point cross-section 49 896, which `leech_truth.py` COUNTS
outright rather than certifying.  `run3` is untouched; `build_system` was lifted out of it so
that the new certifier works on the SAME rows rather than on a reimplementation of them.

The old 361 275 383 was never wrong.  It was what a rounded dual repairs into: 97 short.

## 99. Dimension 38 reaches its ceiling: descent on SO(14), not sampling (2026-08-27)

**tau(38) >= 591 612**, up 24 from 591 588, and the number now matters less than what it is:
it is exactly `3*tau(24) + tau(14) = 589 680 + 1932`, the absolute ceiling of the
equator+caps+axis family in this dimension. There is no slack left in the mechanism at all.

**What was wrong.** The axis layer needs a 60-degree code of R^14 all of whose points lie at
least 30 degrees from the 1932 cap directions. Taking a rotated copy Q.P of the cap direction
set makes the 60-degree condition automatic, so the only question is how many of the 1932
rotated points avoid the caps. The shipped rotation kept 1908, and the README explained the
missing 24 away:

> the 30-degree caps cover about 1.24% of S^13, so a random rotation loses about 24 points
> and best-of-N would recover only a handful

NEITHER clause survives. 1.24% is 24/1932 -- the loss of the one rotation in hand, read back
as if it were a measure of the sphere. The real figure is 2.89%: a 30-degree cap on S^13 has
relative measure 1.4978e-5 and 1932 of them give 2.8937%, whence an expected loss of 55.9,
which is what 200 random rotations actually show (56, sd 11). The independent-points model
therefore gives exp(-56) and not exp(-24) -- sampling is hopeless by a wider margin than the
sentence claimed. The second clause is **a mechanism written down in place of a
measurement**, and it closed the question. It is false: descent walks 1908 -> 1932.

**What works.** SO(14) has 91 dimensions and the objective is differentiable once the maximum
over cap directions is softened:

    S[i,j] = <Q w_i, w_j>,  m_i = softmax_beta_j S[i,j],  f(Q) = sum_i softplus(m_i - 4 sqrt3)

with dS[i,j]/dQ = w_j w_i^T, so the gradient is the 14x14 matrix P^T C^T P and the step
Q <- expm(-eta skew(G Q^T)) Q stays exactly on the group. From the old rotation it walks
1908 -> 1932 in about 200 iterations, in half a minute.

**Getting back to exact arithmetic.** The check is integer arithmetic, so Q must be N/D with
N integer and N N^T = D^2 I. Cayley does that for nothing: (I-S)(I+S)^{-1} is rational and
orthogonal for every rational skew-symmetric S. Take S = (I-Q)(I+Q)^{-1} from the real
solution, round it to (1/d)Z, rebuild in Fractions. Two details decide whether it works:

  * **Descend against a MARGIN.** Rounding moves every inner product, so a solution sitting on
    the threshold does not survive it. The descent targets 4 sqrt3 - 0.15; the rounded
    rotation clears the threshold by 0.113.
  * **d small, and the rounding searched.** D grows like d^14, and round-to-nearest is only
    one of 2^91 roundings. Sweeping d and sampling roundings at each, d = 15 gives
    D = 157 057 717 643 854 109 with all 1932 kept.

**One line of verify.py had to grow.** The old test squared the inner product and compared
with 48 D^2, which forced 48 D^2 < 2^62 and capped D near 3e8 -- ten orders of magnitude
below what a general rotation needs. Squaring is unnecessary: 48 D^2 is not a perfect square,
so 4 sqrt3 D is irrational, and for an INTEGER inner product "<= 4 sqrt3 D" is exactly
"<= isqrt(48 D^2)". One big-integer square root, computed once; everything after it stays in
int64 and needs only 8D < 2^63. The orthogonality check moved to Python integers for the same
reason (D^2 has 35 digits).

**What this does not settle.** tau(14) = 1932 is the best KNOWN kissing number in dimension
14, not a proven one. The ceiling is 3*tau(24) + tau(14) with the TRUE value, so anyone who
improves dimension 14 improves dimension 38 by the same amount -- and nothing else does.

**The general lesson**, which is the reason this section is long: a plausible mechanism that
explains a shortfall will end an investigation as effectively as a proof, and nothing in the
repository can tell the two apart, because both read as prose. "Sampling cannot find it" was
measured. "So it is not there" was not, and did not follow. See also sections 10 and 86, where this construction was built and its
axis layer first analysed.

**Files.** `scripts/axis_rotation.py` re-derives the rotation (a search: it can fail, and a
different numpy may walk a different path -- on the run that shipped it found d = 13 and a
different D, also clean). `scripts/verify.py` checks the SHIPPED N and D exactly, and that is
what the claim rests on.

## 100. The axis layers of 81-95 were built out of the wrong set (2026-08-27)

**+213130 spheres across dimensions 81-95**, and in dimension 86 the axis layer is now AT its
ceiling tau(k).

**The mistake was in the candidate set, not in the search.** A pole at t = 2/3 has to be a
60-degree code and to clear the 30-degree caps around every cap direction. `poles18.py` looked
for it among the vectors OF THE SAME LATTICE, where the shell argument makes the 30-degree
condition automatic: for a of norm M and w of norm m in one lattice, |a-w|^2 and |a+w|^2 are
lattice norms, so |<a,w>| <= M/2 and the condition holds as soon as M <= 3m. Elegant, exact,
and far too small a pool -- at k = 23 it found 124 lines, 248 points, against tau(23) = 93 150.

**Nothing forces the poles to lie in the lattice.** Take them to be a ROTATED COPY of the cap
direction set. The 60-degree condition is then inherited, because a rotation preserves inner
products, and only the 30-degree condition is left -- which is exactly dimension 38's question
(section 99). Here it is far easier: a single 30-degree cap covers 2.284e-8 of S^22, so the
93 150 caps together cover at most 2.128e-3 and a Haar-random rotation loses at most 198 of
the 93 150 in expectation. The rotation shipped loses 106 -- half the bound, the caps
overlapping and the rotation being the best of three. 106 lost out of 93 150, against 248
kept by the old rule.

Write that sentence with care. "A 30-degree cap covers 2.3e-3 of S^22" is false by five
orders of magnitude -- 2.3e-3 is the UNION of 93 150 caps -- and "loses about two hundred"
is the prediction, not the loss, so 93 150 - 200 does not give 93 044. Both errors survived
every check in the repository, and one of them was pinned in place by a factcheck guard
that tested for the literal string 2.3e-3.

| dim | k | shell layer | rotated layer | tau(k) | fraction |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 81 | 9 | 102 | 304 | 306 | 99.3% |
| 82 | 10 | 116 | 464 | 510 | 91.0% |
| 83 | 11 | 0 | 0 | 604 | 0.0% |
| 84 | 12 | 336 | 648 | 841 | 77.1% |
| 85 | 13 | 388 | 904 | 1154 | 78.3% |
| 86 | 14 | 420 | 1932 | 1932 | AT THE CEILING |
| 87 | 15 | 802 | 2340 | 2564 | 91.3% |
| 88 | 16 | 1546 | 4320 | 4320 | AT THE CEILING |
| 89 | 17 | 1228 | 5338 | 5730 | 93.2% |
| 90 | 18 | 1060 | 7374 | 7654 | 96.3% |
| 91 | 19 | 996 | 10648 | 11948 | 89.1% |
| 92 | 20 | 822 | 17380 | 19448 | 89.4% |
| 93 | 21 | 612 | 27682 | 29768 | 93.0% |
| 94 | 22 | 392 | 49852 | 49896 | 99.9% |
| 95 | 23 | 248 | 93074 | 93150 | 99.9% |

**The rotation is rational by construction, so nothing is rounded.** For the metric
C = diag(c) take K an INTEGER skew matrix with entries in {-1,0,1}, put S = C^{-1} K, and
form Q = (I-S)(I+S)^{-1}. Then CS = K is skew, which is exactly the condition for
Q^T C Q = C, so Q is a rational isometry of the form; its denominator D came out with three
to eighteen digits, and

    4<a,z>^2 <= 3 m^2   <=>   2|X| <= isqrt(3 m^2 D^2),    X = (Nw)^T C z

keeps the whole test inside int64 -- the last step because 3 m^2 D^2 is never a perfect
square. No descent, no margin, no rationalising a float: the candidates ARE rational.

**Dimension 86 comes free from dimension 38.** Its cap directions are the same 1932-point
code, vector for vector, so dimension 38's rotation -- the one found by DESCENDING on SO(14),
where sampling stalls at 1908 -- applies verbatim and gives all 1932. `axis_rotate.py` checks
the two sets are equal before borrowing it rather than assuming they are.

**Two traps on the way.**

* `N^T C N = D^2 C`, not `N C N^T`. The two agree only when C is the identity, so the wrong
  one passed at every k except 10, the one metric that is not -- a check that cannot fail on
  most of its inputs is worth very little.
* `Fraction` accepts a numpy int64 and carries it into `.denominator`, so D came back as an
  int64 and silently overflowed. Python ints go in, or the exact path is not exact.

**What is left.** For k = 13, 15, 17, 18, 19 the ceiling tau(k) exceeds Lambda_k's minimal
shell, and a copy cannot be larger than what it copies; for k = 9, 10, 12, 16, 20, 21, 22, 23
the shortfall is the handful a rotation loses to the caps, and section 99 shows that descent
on SO(k) closes exactly that -- affordable at small k, not at k = 23 where one gradient step
is 8.7e9 inner products.

**Dimensions 75-80 followed the same day, through the Cartan matrix.** There the metric is not
diagonal, so the rotation is a G-isometry and S = G^{-1}K rather than C^{-1}K -- the same
construction with diag(c) replaced by the Cartan matrix, which also spares E_6 and E_7 any
embedding in R^8, since a root is just its coordinate vector in the simple-root basis.
Measured against the shell layers, best of 400 integer-skew rotations:

| dim | k | system | shell | rotated | tau(k) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 75 | 3 | A_3 | 8 | 6 | 12 |
| 76 | 4 | D_4 | 24 | 24 | 24 |
| 77 | 5 | D_5 | 28 | **40** | 40 |
| 78 | 6 | E_6 | 58 | **70** | 72 |
| 79 | 7 | E_7 | 76 | **118** | 126 |
| 80 | 8 | E_8 | 174 | **208** | 240 |

+100 spheres, and dimension 77 reaches its ceiling. Below k = 5 the rotation LOSES -- at
k = 3 the 30-degree caps cover 80% of S^2 and a rotated copy keeps 6 where the shell rule
keeps 8 -- so a file is written only where the rotation wins and nothing regresses. That
crossover is the whole story of the two rules: the shell argument is the right tool exactly
where the sphere is small enough for the caps to fill it.

**The general lesson.** The shell argument was correct, exact and beautiful, and it was the
reason nobody looked further: it answered "is this pole legal?" so cleanly that "which poles
are there?" stopped being asked. A sufficient condition that is easy to verify will quietly
become the definition of the search space.


## 101. The naming pass, 2026-08-27

Four sentences, all with correct numbers, all naming the wrong quantity. None was reachable by
any check in the repository at the time, and one was held in place by a check.

**The cap measure.** "A 30-degree cap covers 2.3e-3 of S^22, so a rotation of the 93 150
directions loses about two hundred of them." Measured:

    a single 30-degree cap on S^22          2.284e-8
    the union of the 93 150 caps (bound)    2.128e-3
    the expected loss of a Haar rotation    198.2
    the loss of the rotation shipped        106      -> 93 044 poles

Every number in the sentence was one of these, attached to the wrong noun. 2.3e-3 is the
union wearing the single cap's mantissa; "about two hundred" is the prediction offered as the
measurement. The sentence's own arithmetic does not close -- 93 150 - 200 is not 93 044 --
and it stood in five documents.

This is section 99's error typed a second time, one paragraph later in the same file, the
morning after it was written up. There, 1.24% was 24/1932: a measured loss read back as a
measure of the sphere, when the true figure is 2.89% and the true loss 56. TWO DOCUMENTS
STILL CALLED THAT CLAUSE CORRECT -- this file and the dimension-38 README both said "the
first clause is a measurement and it is right" while paper/README.md said it was not.

The lesson that did not take the first time: a sentence of the form "X covers p of the
sphere, so a rotation of N loses about pN" is ONE calculation with three literals in it, and
writing the three separately is what lets one of them be a different quantity. Guard the
relation. `factcheck.py` now derives p from the integral, pN from p, and the shipped loss
from the .npz, and checks the paper against all three.

**The retained fraction.** "Between 99% and 100% of it" -- the true range is 86.7% (k = 8) to
100%, below 99% at seven of the eighteen k, and above 98% only from k = 12 up. The
sphere-measure argument supports the k >= 12 form and nothing stronger; the general claim was
the large-k behaviour written without its scope.

**tau(20) and tau(21).** `axis_rotate.py` and `verify_poles.py` each carried a private TAU
dict with 17400 and 27720 -- Lambda_20 and Lambda_21 -- both 2048 below the best known, the
best configurations in those two dimensions not being lattices. Nothing computed used them,
so nothing failed. Both files now import common/published.py, and audit check 5n evaluates
every integer-valued `TAU = {...}` in the tree against it, which is falsifiable: plant one and
the audit names the file, the k and both values.

**The count of dimensions.** "Nineteen dimensions -- 75, 77-82 and 84-95" is eighteen, 77-82
and 84-95. Dimension 75 is not among them: at k = 3 the rotation keeps 6 against the shell
rule's 8 and no file was written. 74 and 76 were already at tau(k) and did not move.

**What made all four invisible.** Each is a NAME attached to a computable object -- a measure,
a fraction, a published constant, a set of dimensions -- so each is guardable, and none was
guarded. `factcheck.py` went 598 -> 608 and audit gained 5n; every new check was perturbed
one figure at a time and each caught exactly its own.

**The counts above are historical.** Seven of the layers this section is written against turned out, later the same day, to put the poles outside R^k, and were replaced; section 102 has that. So "the rotation shipped loses 106, leaving 93 044" describes the sentence this section corrected, not the layer that ships. Every number in a post-mortem is a quotation, and quotations go stale in a repository that keeps moving.


## 102. The poles were in the wrong space (2026-08-27)

Seven of the fourteen rotated axis layers written the previous morning put the poles outside
R^k. Dimensions 85, 87, 89, 90, 93, 94 and 95 did not hold as stated. Every inner product in
them was correct, every certificate verified, the whole 48-script suite passed, and the
configurations needed up to 96 dimensions instead of 72+k.

**What the construction needs.** Dimension 72+k puts the cap directions Z and the poles W in
one R^k. Taking W to be a rotated copy of Z makes the 60-degree condition automatic -- but
only if the rotation is an isometry OF R^k.

**What was built.** A Cayley transform of an integer skew matrix on the coordinates Z is
written in. For most k those coordinates are R^k and the construction is sound. For seven of
them they are not:

    k                 13  15  17  18  21  22  23
    Z is written in   14  16  24  24  24  24  24   coordinates
    Z spans           13  15  17  18  21  22  23
    Z and QZ span     14  16  24  24  24  24  24

Lambda_21's cross-section is 27 720 vectors written in Lambda_24's 24 coordinates and
spanning 21 of them. An isometry of R^24 is not an isometry of R^21, and it carried the poles
into three dimensions the configuration does not have.

**Why nothing caught it.** Everything in the repository asked whether the angles were right.
Nothing asked what space the points were in. The exact integer cap test, the isometry test
N^T C N = D^2 C, the pole-pole inheritance argument, the independent Fractions spot-check --
all of them are statements about inner products, and all of them were true. A rotation of the
ambient coordinates preserves every inner product it is asked about. The defect is invisible
to any check that only looks at the Gram matrix.

**The fix, and why the obvious one does not work.** Write W = U P with P a lattice basis of
the span and carry the metric across as G = P C P^T; then every isometry of (Z^k, G) is an
isometry of the axis space. Correct, and unusable: S = G^{-1} K has denominators dividing
det(G), which is 2^59 at k = 17 and 2^71 at k = 23. det(G) is a lattice invariant, so no
choice of basis avoids it, and the exact test overflows int64. Measured, not assumed --
`span_rot.py` found no usable rotation at any of k = 17, 18, 21, 22, 23.

What works is to build the generator out of the direction set. For v, w rows of W,

    S = v (Cw)^T - w (Cv)^T

is integral; CS = (Cv)(Cw)^T - (Cw)(Cv)^T is skew, which is exactly the condition for
Q = (I-S)(I+S)^{-1} to preserve the form; and S sends v to -m w, w to m v and annihilates
everything C-orthogonal to both. So Q fixes the orthogonal complement of the span POINTWISE
and cannot move a pole out of R^k. Over r mutually orthogonal pairs, scaled by 1/lambda, Q is
a direct sum of plane rotations through 2 arctan(m/lambda) and can be written down without
inverting anything:

    N = D I + sum_t [ -2m (v (Cv)^T + w (Cw)^T) + 2 lambda (v (Cw)^T - w (Cv)^T) ],
    D = lambda^2 + m^2

D came out between 80 and 5120 -- against 2^71 -- and the family is BETTER than the one it
replaces at k = 9, 12, 15, 16, 19, 20 and 23, so the correction does not cost spheres at all:
the fourteen cross-section layers come to +62 against the set they replace, and with
the root systems rebuilt as well the whole axis family is +106 on it.

**The guards.** `scripts/verify_poles.py` and audit check 5o both ask it now, and neither is
expensive: Q is linear, so a basis of the span settles it, which is k vectors rather than
93 150. `axis_rotate.py` rejects a candidate that fails, so it can no longer write such a
layer -- at those seven k it now reports that this family has no isometry of R^k and defers
to `scripts/axis_block.py`.

**A second defect, found while fixing the first.** The new script wrote its N as a numpy
OBJECT array, because that is what it computes in. A .npz holding an object array can only be
read back with allow_pickle=True, and verify_poles.py loads without it -- so twelve of the
nineteen layers were unreadable by the very script that certifies them. It failed loudly the
first time it was run, which is the only reason this is a footnote rather than a second
section: had the verifier been run once at the start and trusted thereafter, the package
would have shipped data no user could check. The script now writes int64 and asserts the
values fit; the entries are bounded by D and always do.

**The lesson.** A verification can be exhaustive over the objects it compares and silent
about what those objects are. Every check here compared inner products, so no number of
additional inner-product checks could have found this; what was missing was a check of a
DIFFERENT KIND -- of the ambient space rather than of the geometry inside it. When a
representation carries more coordinates than the object needs, the surplus is a place for an
error to hide where no measurement of the object will look. The question to ask of a data
file is not only "are its numbers right" but "what does it claim to be", and rank is cheap.

## 103. A sweep that gives the same answer twelve times is measuring the wrong thing (2026-08-29)

The axis layers of dimensions 79 and 82 were two and forty-six short of their ceilings:
124 of tau(7) = 126, and 464 of tau(10) = 510. Both are now exact, and so is dimension
81, which was 304 of tau(9) = 306 and fell to the same fix. The fifty spheres are not the
point; the way the shortfall defended itself is.

**Why the search stopped early.** `descend()` maximises HOW MANY of the rotated directions
clear the 30-degree caps. That objective goes flat near the top: a softmax over the caps
sees one bad row against five hundred clear ones, and the gradient it returns is mostly
noise. It reported a plateau, and a plateau in a search is normally read as a property of
the problem. Minimising the WORST cosine instead has a gradient everywhere, and it clears
the whole direction set at both k on the FIRST random start -- with the worst cosine reaching
0.953 of the threshold at k = 7 and 0.977 at k = 10, so not marginally either. The entire
shortfall was in the rational approximation and none of it in the search.

**Then the rational approximation defended itself too, and more convincingly.** At k = 10 the
sweep over denominators returned 430 of 510 at d = 8 and 434 at every one of the twelve larger
values, 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512, 1024, 2048, 4096, 8192, 16384 and 65536. Twelve identical
results spanning three orders of magnitude, and a thirteenth four short of them. The natural
reading is that the denominator is not the binding constraint but something else is -- and the
something else was taken to be the int64 ceiling the exact cap test runs in, which would have
meant a new storage format and a big-integer verification path.

That reading is exactly backwards, and the data says so. **If a sweep gives the same answer
for every value of the swept parameter, the parameter is not what is limiting it.** Twelve
identical results are evidence AGAINST the denominator mattering, not for a ceiling on it.

**What was actually wrong.** The Cayley transform does not cover O(k). From
Q = (I - S)(I + S)^{-1} one gets I + S = 2(I + Q)^{-1}, so S runs to infinity exactly as Q
acquires an eigenvalue of -1 -- and a rotation that lifts a whole direction set clear of the
caps is, of course, one that moves it a long way. The minimax optima sit essentially at that
singularity: |S| came out at 7.4e15 with cond(I + S) = 2.4e17. Every denominator rounded the
same overflowing matrix to the same useless one. The number was printed on the same line as
the sweep and had been looked at without being read.

**The fix is a change of chart.** Pick an integral E with E^T C E = C -- a sign matrix for a
diagonal form, a Weyl element for a Cartan matrix -- and rationalise Q E instead of Q. Same
rotation, different point of the chart, and N = N_0 E is still integral with N^T C N = D^2 C
because E is an isometry of the form. With it, k = 7 reaches 126, k = 9 reaches 306 and k = 10 reaches 510, on
every one of eight random starts, and the denominators come out SMALLER than before rather
than larger: eleven digits at k = 10 against thirteen, and at k = 7 a sampled integer skew
matrix gives **D = 19** against the 2 330 427 630 that shipped.

**Four guards came out of it.** `rationalise()` now always includes the identity chart, so the
search contains the one it replaces and cannot return less. `cleared()` now enforces the two
limb bounds `verify_poles.py` asserts, so the search cannot produce a layer the certificate
script would refuse. The two limits are different and neither dominates: at k = 3 the verifier
stops at a denominator of about 3e10 while the search's own guard allows 1e18, and at k = 10
and 14 it runs the other way about. That is measured, per k; the first version of this sentence
guessed where the gap was and put it at the one k where it does not exist. And `improve()`
stops once the layer is all of Z, since a copy of Z cannot exceed Z; that is what makes re-
running this affordable at all, the descent it skips being an hour of arithmetic at k = 10. A
fourth, found while timing the re-run: at k = 13, 15, 17, 18, 21, 22 and 23 the direction set
is written in more coordinates than it spans, so this family has no isometry of R^k at all and
every candidate is rejected. The file said so -- but only by way of "sampling produced nothing
valid", which is true only when the SHIPPED rotation is invalid too. When axis_block.py made
those layers valid on 2026-08-27 the branch stopped firing, and the file began running a full
descent against a family that provably cannot beat what is already there: nine minutes at k =
13 to return its input. It now computes the rank first and says so. A guard whose precondition
was the defect it guarded against disappears the moment the defect is fixed.

**And it removed the paper's last assumption.** Table 3 calibrates the counting formula
against the seven published values of dimensions 25-31, and each column needs a set of K(k)
poles 30 degrees from every cap direction. Six were exhibited; the k = 7 column assumed one,
because the search reached 124 of 126. It is exhibited now, so no column of that table
assumes anything. Two spheres in dimension 79 are worth nothing; the assumption they were
hiding was worth removing.

**The lesson, in one line.** An invariance is a measurement. Twelve identical answers said
"not this parameter" as clearly as any error message, and the reason it took a day to hear
is that the invariance was being used as evidence for a conclusion it contradicted.


## 104. The poles never had to be a copy of the caps (2026-08-29)

The axis layer of dimension 72+k is a set `W` of unit vectors of `R^k`, and the construction
asks exactly two things of it: 60 degrees from each other, 30 degrees from every cap
direction. Nothing asks it to be a rotated copy of the cap set `Z`. But that is what
`scripts/axis_rotate.py` builds, and the consequence is a ceiling of `|Z|` that no rotation
can beat. In eight of these dimensions `Z` is the minimal-vector set of `Lambda_k` while
`tau(k)` is larger, so those layers were short **by construction** -- 6913 spheres, recorded
here as "not a search problem" and left. It was not a geometry problem either. The ansatz
was the ceiling.

**Three of the eight were the same object in disguise.** `data/lam19_W.npy` and
`lam20_W.npy` are, after halving, integer vectors of norm 8 in exactly `k` coordinates, of
two shapes: `4 C(n,2)` vectors `(+-2,+-2,0,...)` and 128 sign patterns on each octad of a
shortened Golay code, always with an EVEN number of minus signs. That is not merely
`Lambda_k`; it is the coordinate frame Cohn-Li work in, and their record is the same
configuration with the octad signs taken ODD, plus one `+-1` vector for every word of the
dual octad code. The record configuration of `R^19` and `R^20` was already sitting in the
package's own data files, one sign flip away.

**And the 30-degree condition is far weaker than the 60-degree one.** Unrotated, in the
halved frame, an odd-sign octad vector meets a cap at `|X| <= 6` against a limit of 6, and a
`+-1` vector at `|X| <= 8` against a limit of 10. Both families clear every cap with NO
ROTATION AT ALL. Only the `4 C(n,2)` pair vectors fail, and they fail completely -- they ARE
cap directions, so all 684 of them sit at zero degrees. That alone gives 11264 poles at
k = 19 against the 10 648 the rotated copy gave, with denominator 1. A rotation moves the
pair vectors off their own caps and is worth the rest.

**k = 21 needed the configuration carried in, and whether that is possible is a question
about the space.** There the caps are 27720 vectors written in 24 coordinates spanning 21,
so a configuration built in `R^21` has to be mapped in by a rational similarity `M` with
`M^T M = mu I` -- `k` orthogonal vectors of a common norm in the span. Existence is decided
by the DISCRIMINANT: the determinant of any Gram of the span, modulo rational squares, is the
product of the norms of any orthogonal basis, so a frame of common norm `mu` forces
`disc = mu^k`. Computed for every k here:

| k | 12 | 13 | 15 | 16 | 17 | 18 | 19 | 20 | 21 | 22 | 23 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| disc | 1 | 2 | 1 | 1 | 2 | **3** | 1 | 1 | 1 | **3** | 2 |

At k = 18 and 22 the rank is even, so `mu^k` is a square while the discriminant is 3: **no
frame exists at all**, and those two axis spaces cannot hold a rational copy of anything
built in the standard `R^k`. That is a fact about the space, not about the search. At k = 21
the discriminant is 1, so `mu` must be a SQUARE -- which is why a greedy frame over the cap
directions, of norm 32, stops at 20 of 21 however long it runs, and why one built from
norm-4 vectors of the span completes on the first try.

The README already recorded that a lattice basis of the span is unusable, its denominators
dividing a `det(G)` of `2^71`. The frame sidesteps that: in frame coordinates the metric is
`mu I`, of determinant `mu^k`, and the Cayley transform has the small denominators it has
everywhere else. The two statements are about different families and both hold.

**Dimension 83 was a ring problem and not a geometry problem, and it said so.** Its 604 cap
directions live over `Z[sqrt2]`, and the paper recorded `W = empty` with the reason "the
rational rotations above are not set up for it". Only the CONFIGURATION is irrational; the
ROTATION need not be. With `Q = N/D` rational and `N^T C N = D^2 C` the poles stay in the
same ring, and the cap condition is one comparison in `Z[sqrt2]` per pair --
`(972 D^2 - p^2 - 2 q^2) - 2 p q sqrt2 >= 0`, decided by two signs and `a^2` against `2 b^2`.
The first random start cleared all 604, at 0.9435 of the threshold.

**What it came to: +5 955 spheres.**

| dim | k | layer before | layer now | tau(k) | spheres |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 83 | 11 | 0 | 604 | 604 | +604 |
| 91 | 19 | 10 648 | 11 934 | 11 948 | +1 286 |
| 92 | 20 | 17 380 | 19 415 | 19 448 | +2 035 |
| 93 | 21 | 27 682 | 29 712 | 29 768 | +2 030 |

**Two defects in the checking machinery, both found by this.** `audit.py` recomputes every
claim from the shipped certificates rather than from the drivers, which is the point of it --
and it read only `poles_rot_<k>.npz`, so it recomputed dimensions 83, 91 and 92 at their old
values and then AGREED with a RESULTS.md that was equally stale. Two stale things agreeing is
exactly what an independent recomputation exists to prevent. And its summary line printed
"RESULTS.md agrees with every recomputed claim" unconditionally, on the line after listing the
dimensions where it did not.

**The lesson is about where a ceiling comes from.** "A rotated copy of `Z` cannot exceed
`|Z|`" is true, it was written down here, and it reads like a statement about the geometry.
It is a statement about the ANSATZ. What to ask of a ceiling is not how tight it is but which
of the problem's own conditions it follows from -- and this one followed from none of them:
the two inequalities the construction imposes on `W` never mention `Z` except as something to
stay 30 degrees away from.

## 105. The obstruction was to the metric, not to the configuration (2026-08-30)

Section 104 dropped the ansatz that the poles must be a rotated copy of the caps, and took
the record configuration of `R^k` as the pole source at k = 19, 20 and 21. It left 1562
spheres on the table and a reason for each. Four of those reasons were the same one, and it
was wrong.

**The configurations were on disk the whole time.** At k = 13, 15, 17 and 18 the record
configuration is not of the odd-octad shape and cannot be rebuilt from the Golay code, which
is why section 104 stopped there. It does not have to be rebuilt. Cohn's
`dimensions1-24.txt` -- the same data set five packages here already read -- gives every
dimension 1 to 24 as exact integer coordinates with an integer metric `diag(c)`. The
package had been treating that file as a source of CAP directions, where the record loses to
the lattice because its triple structure is too thin; as a source of POLES, where no
partition into triples is asked of anything, it wins by 200 to 400 points per dimension.

**The k = 18 obstruction was real and was about the standard metric.** Section 104 recorded:
the axis space of dimension 90 has discriminant 3 and even rank, so `mu^18` is a square, no
frame of one norm exists, and no configuration built in the standard `R^18` can be placed
there rationally. Every word of that is true. It is also not about the configuration. A
frame satisfies `M^T M = mu diag(c)` for the source's OWN metric, so the condition reads
`mu^k det(diag c) = disc` modulo squares -- and Cohn's data set writes the record
configuration of `R^18` in `diag(1^16, 2, 6)`, of discriminant 12 = 3. Which is the
discriminant of the axis space. The frame exists.

Its columns are `v_1 +- v_2, ..., v_15 +- v_16` of norm 64 from mutually orthogonal cap
directions, `2 v_17` of norm 128, and for the last one the orthogonal complement of those
seventeen inside the span. That complement is a LINE, so its norm class is forced, not
chosen: the frame needs 6 modulo squares and the line has norm 6. At k = 15 the frame needs
1 and the line has norm 4. Two chances to be wrong, taken twice, and right both times --
which is the strongest evidence that the discriminant argument is the right one.

**A full-rank Cayley transform cannot be rounded finely enough here.** Its denominator is
about `d^k`, so at rank 17 a usable `D` allows `d = 4`, which is 12 per cent per entry
against margins of about 1 per cent. The rotation is built instead as a product of rational
PLANE rotations of `diag(c)`: in the plane of two frame axes with weights `g_i`, `g_j`,

    R = [[a, -(g_j/g_i) b], [b, a]] / D,     a^2 + (g_j/g_i) b^2 = D^2,

which has denominator `D` alone, and whose angles are the rational points of a conic --
dense in the circle. One of them, with `D = 13`, is worth more than the entire shortfall at
k = 15 and 17. The final denominators are 65, 425, 65, 715.

> **The k = 13 row is SUPERSEDED by section 106, the same day.**  Dimension 85's cap set
> changed from Lambda_13 to the Kappa section K_13, which is larger and splits perfectly into
> zero-sum triples; the frame below does not survive that change and cannot be made to, since
> the record configuration of R^13 is written in the standard metric and K_13's span carries
> diag(3,1,3,1,3,1,3,1,3,1,3,1,1), whose Hasse invariants differ at 2 and at 3 (rank 13 is
> odd, so mu fixes the discriminant and cannot touch the Hasse invariant).  **The shipped
> layer at k = 13 is 918**, a rotated copy of K_13, and the trade was worth 32 350 spheres
> against the 224 poles it cost.  The other three rows stand.

| dim | k | layer before | layer now | tau(k) | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 85 | 13 | 904 | 1106 | 1154 | the record configuration of R^13, through a frame (1106 of its 1154 points) -- SUPERSEDED, see above |
| 87 | 15 | 2340 | 2562 | 2564 | the record configuration of R^15, through a frame |
| 89 | 17 | 5338 | 5704 | 5730 | the record configuration of R^17, through a frame |
| 90 | 18 | 7374 | 7624 | 7654 | the record configuration of R^18, through a frame |

That is **+1040 spheres**, in dimensions 85, 87, 89, 90. Dimension 85 uses the 1106 points of the
1154 that the data set writes with rational coordinates; the other 48 carry a `2 sqrt 3` and
are not placed. The other three sources are complete.

**A greedy that stops at the first round where nothing improves is not solving this
problem.** Two plane rotations can gain where neither gains alone, so the search takes a
SIDEWAYS move when no improving one exists and keeps the best PREFIX of the accepted
sequence rather than its end. At k = 15 that is the difference between 2552 and 2562, ten
poles from one change to the stopping rule -- the same family of mistake as section 103's
plateau, one level up: there the objective was flat, here the acceptance rule was greedy.

**The lesson.** Section 104's lesson was that a ceiling can belong to the ansatz. This one
is narrower and sharper: an obstruction stated for one presentation of an object is not an
obstruction to the object. "No configuration built in the standard `R^18` can be placed
there rationally" was carefully scoped and still read, for a day, as "dimension 90 is
closed" -- including by the person who wrote it. Ask of every impossibility proof what it
quantifies over, and then ask whether the thing you want is inside that range.

## 106. The cap set was never forced, and two partitions were not perfect (2026-08-30)

Section 105 took the cap DIRECTIONS as given and worked on the poles.  That was the smaller
half of the problem by three orders of magnitude, and I had been reading past the larger one
for eleven sections: `verify_parts.py` states in its own docstring what the construction asks
of the direction set W -- distinct vectors, spanning k dimensions, a 60-degree code, and a
partition into parts internally at cosine <= -1/2 -- and none of that says WHICH code.

**The cap set at k = 12 and 13 was not the largest available, and the repository said so.**
`final81-95.py` carries a table `ANTIP_FULL` of the best LATTICE kissing numbers, used only
to check that the triple scheme beats antipodal pairs.  It reads 756 at k = 12 and 918 at
k = 13 -- and the shipped direction sets were Lambda_12's 648 and Lambda_13's 906.
`research/dim81-95/derive.py` even names the two lattices, `K_12` and `K_13`.  A triple is
worth 4 per class line, a class is about 2100 lines, so one missing triple is 8400 spheres,
and 36 of them were missing at k = 12.

**Why they were not used, and why that reason was wrong.**
`research/dim81-95/richspan2.py` is a greedy hunt for a twelve-dimensional subspace of the
Leech holding 756 minimal vectors.  It did not find one, and that closed the question.  It
was the wrong question: a subspace like that is not FOUND, it is the fixed space of a
SYMMETRY.  Let pi be an automorphism of the Golay code of cycle shape 1^6 3^6 -- an element
of order 3 in M_24 -- acting on the Leech by permuting coordinates.  Six fixed points and six
3-cycles give twelve orbits, so its fixed space is twelve-dimensional, and so is the
orthogonal complement.  On the COMPLEMENT pi has no eigenvalue 1, so

    I + pi + pi^2 = 0   there,

and every orbit {v, pi v, pi^2 v} of a vector of the complement SUMS TO ZERO.  The 756
minimal vectors of the Leech lying in it have determinant 3^6 at minimal norm 4, which is the
Coxeter-Todd lattice K_12, and their partition into 252 zero-sum triples is the orbit set
of pi.  Not a packing search: a group acting.  Both halves hold 756; the complement is the
one to use, because there the partition is free.

**K_13 is one direction more, and it is not searched for either.**  A Leech minimal vector
lies in span(K_12) + Ru exactly when its component orthogonal to span(K_12) is parallel to u,
so project all 196560 onto the complement of the span and take the largest parallel class:
378 classes tie at 162, and 756 + 162 = 918.  Thirteen is odd, so no order-3 isometry of
R^13 acts freely -- one must fix a vector -- and those last 54 triples ARE packed.

**In exactly k coordinates.**  A rotated copy of the cap set is built by a Cayley transform
on the coordinates W is written in, and that is an isometry of the AXIS SPACE only when W has
full rank in them; 756 vectors written in 24 coordinates span 12.  Both sets reduce, because
the complement of Fix(pi) is the direct sum over the six 3-cycles of the plane x + y + z = 0,
and that plane has the ORTHOGONAL integer basis (1,-1,0) of norm 2 and (1,1,-2) of norm 6.
Integrality needs x = y mod 2, which holds for a reason worth keeping: the (-3, 1^23) Leech
vectors have every coordinate odd, and three odd numbers cannot sum to zero, so that shape
never meets the complement at all.  K_12 comes out in diag(1,3)^6 at norm 16, K_13 in
diag(3,1,3,1,3,1,3,1,3,1,3,1,1) at norm 48.

**The same identity settles four partitions that the packing search had left short.**  An
isometry S with I + S + S^2 = 0 has eigenvalues omega and omega-bar only, so it acts freely
and all its orbits are zero-sum triples; an S-invariant W is therefore partitioned perfectly
by them.  Those eigenvalues come in conjugate pairs, so this can only happen in EVEN
dimension -- k = 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19, 21 and 23 are ruled out by that alone.  At k = 16, 18,
20 and 22 such an S exists, and `lamtriples.py`'s greedy had found 1434 triples of a
possible 1440, 2457 of 2466, 5782 plus 23 pairs of a possible 5800, and 16594 plus 49 of
a possible 16632.  Six, nine, eighteen and thirty-eight triples.  20820, 29092, 53794 and
110916 spheres.

CONSISTENCY ON A BASIS IS NOT SET-PRESERVATION, and the search was wrong about that for an
hour.  Choosing S(v) = w forces S(w) = -v-w, and every later image must be an isometry
against all of them, which collapses a space of 82^k assignments to a few dozen nodes -- but
the first complete assignment reached is set-preserving at k = 18 and is NOT at k = 12 or 16,
where the search has to continue to the 18th and the 77th.  A version that stopped at the
first node reported "found" at k = 16 and produced a map that did not permute the cap set.

| dim | k | cap set | triples | poles | before | now | gain |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 84 | 12 | K_12, 756 vectors | 252 | 756 | 6219991556 | 6220293304 | +301748 |
| 85 | 13 | K_13, 918 vectors | 306 | 918 | 6220712146 | 6220745414 | +33268 |
| 88 | 16 | Lambda_16, unchanged | 1440 | unchanged | 6230185672 | 6230206492 | +20820 |
| 90 | 18 | Lambda_18, unchanged | 2466 | unchanged | 6238715696 | 6238744788 | +29092 |
| 92 | 20 | Lambda_20, unchanged | 5800 | unchanged | 6266351873 | 6266405667 | +53794 |
| 94 | 22 | Lambda_22, unchanged | 16632 | unchanged | 6355628684 | 6355739600 | +110916 |

That is **+549638 spheres**, and it is 528 times section 105's +1040.

**A LAYER BELONGS TO A CAP SET, and nothing numeric catches it when it does not.**  An axis
layer is a rotation N/D together with a set of INDICES into W.  Both halves are meaningless
against a different W: the indices point at vectors that may not be there.  Until today each
k had exactly one cap set ever chosen for it and this could not go wrong.  It went wrong
within the hour: `audit.py` crashed multiplying a 14-coordinate rotation of Lambda_13 by a
13-coordinate cap set, which is the loud version.  The quiet version is a 648-pole layer read
against a 756-vector set, which is a perfectly well-typed answer to a question nobody asked.
So `scripts/capsha.py` now stamps each layer with the digest of the cap set it was built
from, readers check it, the Kappa layers are named apart (`poles_kap_<k>.npz`), and the seven
Lambda_12 and Lambda_13 artefacts were retired rather than left on disk to be checked against
the wrong configuration.

**What was measured and did NOT work**, so it is not tried again:

* The record configuration of R^k as the CAP set at k = 15, 17, 18, 19, 20.  It is larger
  than Lambda_k -- 2564 against 2340, 5730 against 5346 -- and it is full of zero-sum
  triples: 17016, 66432, 99202, 147528, 242080 of them.  It packs terribly.  The same greedy
  that finds Lambda_15's perfect 780 finds 518 of a possible 854 there, 482 of 1910 at
  k = 17, 684 of 3982 at k = 19; with the leftovers paired the units are 1521, 3081 and 6301
  against Lambda_k's 1560, 3557 and 7102.  Lambda_k wins everywhere.  The best case had been
  worth +17.4 million and the measurement closed it in twenty minutes.
* A perfect triple partition of dimension 83's 604-point configuration would be worth
  +244058.  3000 restarts of the packing all return 84 triples, the shipped number.  An
  invariance across a sweep is a measurement: 84 is the ceiling, not the search.
* Splitting an odd k into an even sub-configuration with an omega plus a packed remainder.
  At k = 17 the largest hyperplane section is exactly Lambda_16's 4320, giving 1440 free
  triples -- and the remaining 1026 vectors hold only 512 zero-sum triples, of which 2 can be
  packed.  1442 against the 1773 the plain greedy already finds.  At k = 21 the section is
  12462, so 4154 free triples, and the remaining 15258 pack to 1944: 6098 against 9215.  At
  k = 23, 43164 gives 14388 free and the remaining 49986 pack to 4928: 19316 against 30990.
  Three measurements, each losing by a third or more.  The free part is bought by giving up
  the triples that CROSS the section, and those are most of the partition.
* Nothing here, in the end.  Lambda_20 fell once the search filtered candidate images by
  TRIPLE-DEGREE -- S carries zero-sum triples to zero-sum triples, so it preserves the
  number of them through a vector, and at k = 20 those degrees take three values, which
  takes the branching from 664 to eight and finds the isometry in 607 complete assignments.
  At k = 22 the degree is CONSTANT at 1492 and the filter buys nothing -- and the isometry
  condition settles it there anyway, in TWELVE complete assignments.  What remains open is
  the odd k: 17, 19, 21 and 23 cannot carry such an S at all, and their partitions are
  9, 13, 25 and 60 triples short of perfect, worth about 200000 spheres between them.
  **Section 107 closes all four, for 286996; the estimate here was 30% low.**

**The lesson.**  Section 104's was that a ceiling can belong to the ansatz; section 105's was
that an impossibility can belong to a presentation.  This one is smaller and more
embarrassing than either: I spent two sections optimising the poles, which are worth 1 sphere
each, while the cap set next to them was worth 8400 a triple and had been chosen once, early,
by a script that asked whether a subspace could be FOUND.  Before optimising a term, price
every term.


## 107. The greedy stopped at a MAXIMAL packing, and the move that fixes it is forced (2026-08-30)

Section 106 closed the even k with a group and left the odd ones open: 17, 19, 21 and 23 were
9, 13, 25 and 60 triples short of perfect, written up as "worth about 200000 spheres between
them".  All four are now perfect and the figure is **286996** -- the estimate was loose
because I had not computed it, which is section 106's fault one layer down.

**Why a greedy stops short, and what the right move is.**  `lamtriples.py` places triples one
at a time and never revisits one, so it stops at a MAXIMAL packing rather than a maximum one.
The fix is a local search, and here the move set is not a design decision: it is forced.  The
hypergraph of zero-sum triples is LINEAR -- two vectors lie in at most one triple, since the
third is forced to be -(a+b).  Take a triangle {u,v,w} with u uncovered.  If v and w belonged
to a common packed triple t, then t's third vector would be -(v+w) = u, which is uncovered --
so they never do.  Evicting the triples that meet {u,v,w} therefore frees either 3 vectors
(one eviction) or 6 (two), and ONE eviction leaves the uncovered count unchanged.  One
eviction happens exactly when one of v, w is itself uncovered, so

    every neutral or improving move is a PAIR OF UNCOVERED VECTORS at 120 degrees,

and with |U| in the tens that is a few hundred pairs, enumerated in one pass.  Sampling a
random triangle through a random uncovered vector is looking for a needle -- at k = 23 each
vector lies in 1232 triples and all but a handful lead nowhere -- and the sampling version of
the same walk stalls two or three triples short where the enumerating one closes.

**The result.**  All four shells partition perfectly:

    k    |W|    perfect   was            now      spheres
    17   5346   1782      1773 T + 11 P  1782 T   +29120    dimension 89
    19  10668   3556      3543 T + 16 P  3556 T   +41500    dimension 91
    21  27720   9240      9215 T + 34 P  9240 T   +66048    dimension 93
    23  93150   31050     30990 T + 82 P 31050 T  +150328   dimension 95

286996 in total.

**And the last of them turned on a PARAMETER I had read as a fact about the lattice.**  The
pair scan is quadratic in |U|, so the walk enumerates below some threshold and samples above
it.  The first value I wrote was 150.  At 150, k = 23 stalls at 31047 or 31048 -- through cold
restarts, through iterated local search with kicks of up to forty triples, through a tabu on
recently placed triples, and through seeding the walk with Lambda_22's perfect partition,
which sits inside Lambda_23 as the level-0 set of the functional orthogonal to its span.  Four
strategies, one answer, and I had already written "k = 23 did not close" into three documents
before trying the threshold.  At 800 it closes in 55 seconds.  Sampling once a packing is
nearly full is exactly where sampling is worst, and 150 was switching to it far too early.
An invariance across four strategies is a measurement of what they share, and what they shared
was the constant.

**The trap: the obvious economy is not available.**  W = -W and a triple's signs are fixed up
to a global one -- a+b+c = 0 and a-b+c = 0 would give b = 0 -- so triples come in antipodal
pairs and the problem looks like it halves.  I spent an hour on the LINE version before
noticing that it caps at 890 of 891 at k = 17 and will not move.  It is not the search.
Summing [a] + [b] + [c] = 0 in Lambda / 2 Lambda over an antipodally symmetric perfect
partition gives

    sum over the |W|/2 minimal LINES of [v] = 0   in Lambda / 2 Lambda,

one lattice membership test, and it FAILS for Lambda_k at k = 11, 13, 15, 17, 19 and 23.  On
the vectors the same sum is doubled and the condition is vacuous.  So at those k no perfect
partition is antipodally symmetric -- which rules out an order-3 isometry there for a second,
independent reason, since an S-invariant partition IS symmetric (the orbit of -v is the
negative of the orbit of v).  It does not rule one out at k = 21, where the test passes and
the eigenvalue argument does the work instead.  `oddparts.py` prints the test in a `sym`
column.

**A bug the same work exposed: 169 of the 176 pair parts at k = 11 were not parts.**  The
604-point record configuration of R^11 lives in Z[sqrt2], and `verify11.py` decides
cos <= -1/2 as an inequality between integers.  Its docstring wrote the condition as
`-(P + N/2) >= Q sqrt2`, which is the right inequality with its sign REVERSED, and the code
matched the docstring -- under an `or` whose other branch is a condition on the CONJUGATE
P - Q sqrt2 and is satisfied by everything in that Gram.  Both branches passed 161 pairs at
cos = +1/2 and 8 at cos = 0.  A check that cannot fail on the data reads as coverage; this one
could not fail at all.

The units 2T + P = 344 were nevertheless right.  CP-SAT maximising 2T + P over the triples and
the admissible pairs returns 344 as the EXACT optimum in 26 seconds, and maximising T alone
returns 84 in one, so dimension 83's number does not move and section 106's "3000 restarts all
return 84" is now a theorem rather than an invariance.  `scripts/k11_optimal.py` ships both
solves; it needs OR-Tools and is not part of `run_all.py`.  The certificate is replaced by an optimal one
whose 176 pairs are genuine -- 172 at cos = -1/2 and 4 antipodal -- and the check is now shown
to be able to fail, on a cos = +1/2 and a cos = 0 witness drawn from the same Gram, before it
is trusted.

**And a measurement that did not move.**  Section 106 rejected the record configurations as
CAP sets on a greedy's word.  Re-measured with this walk: 520 triples against 508 at k = 15,
482 against 482 at k = 17, 1496 against 1475 at k = 18.  At k = 17 the ceiling is structural --
384 of the 5730 points lie in no zero-sum triple at all, so nothing can exceed 1782 there --
and the lattice keeps the column by hundreds of units.  That rejection stands, and now stands
on a measurement of the configurations rather than of the packer.

**The lesson.**  Section 106's was to price every term.  This one is about the SHAPE of a
search rather than its effort.  Three sessions of restarts had been thrown at these partitions
and every one of them re-ran the same move -- draw a triangle, hope it fits.  Nothing about
the object was ever asked.  One line of reasoning about linearity says which moves exist, and
once the move set is right they close in seconds.  Before adding restarts, derive the move --
and before believing the result, vary the one constant that decides which move you are using.

## 108. Release day: what had no guard was the description of the artefact (2026-08-30)

The package was read end to end before being pushed, and every defect found was in prose
ABOUT the repository rather than in the mathematics.  That is not a coincidence and it is the
whole content of this section.

**Three numbers describing this repository had gone stale together, and all three were in the
first thing a reader does.**  `README.md`'s "Reproducing everything" block said
`python run_all.py  # 19 scripts, about 7 minutes` against a table of 53 taking 28, said
`--full  # 24 scripts, about an hour` against 70, and the root README described the
deliverable as "~300 files / 37 MB" against 359.  `run_all.py`'s own docstring had been kept
current -- because a measured run is pasted into it -- but the two READMEs quoting it had not,
because nothing read them.  Every mathematical figure in this repository is guarded, several
of them relationally; not one guard looked at the sentence that tells somebody how to run it.
`audit.py` check **5s** now imports `run_all.JOBS` and walks the tree under its own
`.gitignore`, so both numbers are measured rather than read.

**The same failure, one level up: a table row named the wrong lattice.**  Section 97a's table
of `alpha` bottlenecks carried the row `| Gamma_72 class, 248 lines | ... | 425.45 | 248 |`.
Every digit in it is right and every digit is the LEECH's: `425.45 = 4680/11` is Cohn-Jiao-
Kumar-Torquato's bound on any line system in `R^24` with `|cos| <= 1/4`, and 248 is Ma et
al.'s record in it.  Gamma_72's class problem -- the one that moves 23 dimensions -- is the
paragraph BELOW that table, and it has no bound of any kind.  Section 101 was written about
exactly this failure mode (a name wrong for weeks while every number checked out) and it had
survived one row above a paragraph that contradicts it.

**Release-day re-read of everything this repository is a function of.**  Four claims are
`external` -- 39, 62, 63 and 96 -- and each is a function of a published table that moves.
All were read again on the day of release: `A(38,8,8) = 3025` and `A(39,8,8) = 3324` on
Brouwer's page, `[62,26]` and `[63,27]` still at `d = 16` with `[62,27]` and `[63,28]` still
at 15 on codetables.de, `[96,33]` still at `d = 24` with `[96,34]` only 22, and `[24,10]_4`
still at `d = 11` so `[24,9,12]_4` is still the best grid map.  Cohn's table was re-read at
every row any claim or withdrawal here touches -- 25, 27, 38, 39, 44, 45, 46, 47, 48 and 72 --
and all ten agreed.  Nothing moved.  The point of recording a null result here is that the
next person can see the date, not just the value.

**And a question the paper had never answered: why does the table stop at 96?**  It said why
the LAYERED construction stops winning at 96, which is a different question -- that is why 96
belongs to Edel-Rains-Sloane, not why 97 is absent.  The answer is that `K` is superadditive:
two 60-degree codes on orthogonal subspaces meet at inner product 0, so
`K(96 + m) >= K(96) + K(m)`, and every dimension above 96 already follows from the row for 96
and a published low-dimensional record.  A row for dimension 100 reading `12886999232 + 24`
would be arithmetic on the row above it.

What is NOT obvious is whether something overtakes that implied floor above 96 the way ERS
overtakes Gamma_72 AT 96, and that had to be measured rather than asserted.  The layered
construction cannot: it ends at `k = 24`.  The chain can, but not by enough to matter --
evaluated through `n = 128` from the same tables used everywhere here, it exceeds the implied
floor by at most **0.016%**, at `n = 114`, where the floor is `K(96) + K(18)`.
`closed/dim96-ers-takeover/above96.py` is that computation, and it fails if the excess ever
reaches one per cent, at which point the range would have to be reconsidered.  It also proves
its own comparison can go red, by re-running it against half the floor.

**`python audit.py` FAILED on a clean checkout, and only a clean checkout could show it.**
The tau(k)-exact clause of check 5r scans `../paper/README.md` and
`../notes/SUBMISSION-all-dimensions.md` for the sentence naming the dimensions whose axis
layer reaches tau(k), and treats "no document states it" as a defect.  Both documents are the
author's working folder, not the deliverable.  In a clone of `kissing_verifications` alone
neither exists, so the audit reported a failure for a sentence that was not there to be
wrong -- and `python audit.py` is the FIRST command the README tells a reviewer to run.  It
now stands down and says which it is doing, as checks 5h and 5s already did.

That fault has now occurred twice, in two checks written months apart, and both times it was
found by copying the package somewhere else and running it rather than by reading it.  **A
check that reads outside the package must be written to notice that the outside is not
there.**  The rule for this repository: after adding any check that opens a path beginning
`..`, run the audit from a copy of `kissing_verifications` alone before believing it.

**A guarantee stated wider than the check behind it.**  `.gitattributes` said "no file mixes
them within itself" and check 5k printed "none mixed".  The scan opens ten extensions --
`.py .md .txt .cff .json .tex .bib .yml .cfg .out` -- and the tree holds one file that does
mix and is invisible to it, the archived copy of Cohn's page in
`closed/dim32-44-ers-audit/data/`.  The right repair is not to widen the check until it fails
on a downloaded artefact -- normalising an archive changes the thing that was archived, and
`* -text` preserves its bytes anyway -- but to write the scope into the sentence, which is
now done in both places.  Section 105's lesson at the level of a guard: an impossibility, or
a guarantee, has a scope, and the scope belongs in the statement.

**And one character was from the wrong alphabet.**  The submission notes wrote the lattice as
the subscripted `Gamma_72` with a Cyrillic Ghe U+0413 where every other occurrence in the project has a Greek Gamma
U+0393.  The two render identically in every font here, so proofreading cannot see it -- and
a grep for Gamma does not find the sentence, so the usual way of checking that a name is used
consistently reports the file clean.  The author types on a Russian layout, so this is a
standing hazard rather than an accident.  `audit.py` check **5l** now flags an ISOLATED
Cyrillic letter anywhere in a `.md` or `.tex`; runs of Cyrillic are left alone, because a
Russian journal title is quoted legitimately in section 33 of this file.  This is section
101's lesson again -- a name wrong while every number is right -- at the level of the
character rather than the word.

**The lesson.**  Guards get built where the errors were, and the errors were in the claims, so
the claims are guarded to the digit.  Nothing was built to guard the sentences that say how
big the thing is, how long it takes, or which object a table row is about -- and those are
what a reader meets first and what a reviewer quotes back.  The rule that generalises: **a
number describing the artefact needs a guard exactly as much as a number describing the
mathematics, and it is more likely to go stale, because nothing downstream depends on it.**
